FOREWORD TO THE REVISED EDITION

Th* Export and Import Policy announced an the 31st 
March, 1992 Is effective for a period of five years from 1st 
April, 1992 to 31st March, 1997 and, will co- terminate with 
the Eighth Plan period, Since the announcement of this 
Policy,, tbc Imports and Exports (Control) Act. 1947, has 
been replaced by the Foreign Trade (Development and Regu¬ 
lation) Act, 1992 and the Chief Controller of Imports and 
Exports has been rc-designatcd as Director General of For¬ 
eign Trade. The provisions of the new Act and the new 
designation reflect in more clear terms tho objectives and 
the thiust of the current Policy towards promotion and de¬ 
velopment of Foreign Tilldc instead of controls and regula¬ 
tions. 

2. At the, time of the announcement of the Policy, it was 
also expressed that the basic philosophy cif the Policy is to 
regulate, wherever necessary, exports and imports through 
the tariff mechanism by abolishing the licensing restrictions 
and discretionary controls. This has been achieved substan¬ 
tially during the course of the year. The pace of liberali¬ 
sation has been stepped up and further efforts in this direct¬ 
ion arc envisaged during tho coming years. 

3. Certain changes have been made in tho Policy effective 
from 1st April, 1993 for giving farther fillip to tho export 
efforts and for facilitating bettor and smooth administration 
of various schemes; making them more attractive and trans¬ 
parent by eliminating ambiguity and procedural constraints. 
Some of the changes trade in the Policy are : 

(i) The principal objectives of the current Export and 
import Policy have been redefined with a view to 
enhance export capabilities of the agriculture and 
service sectors also by promoting productivity, 
modernisation and competitiveness thereof; 

(ii) The definition of ‘Capital Goods' has been enlarged 
mi as to bring vvirhih- its ambit Various sector* 
like agriculture, mining and services. This will in¬ 
crease the scope of Imports of Capjtal goods at 
concessional rate of Customs duty and help in ach¬ 
ieving export : targets in these sectors, 

(hi) A new scheme for grant of Export Pronto'' -n Cap’- 
tal Goods licences for -the, services sector is being 
launched, which will enable the professional) like 
archit ects, doctors, diagnostic centre*, economists, 
jouranmts, lawyers etc., to import specified cquip- 


(vjii) In respect of EOU/EPZ Units in agriculture and 
allied sectors, DTA sales have been permitted upto 
50% of their value of production subject to fulfil¬ 
ment of minimum value addition and export obli¬ 
gation. Furtlicr, the formula of value addition for 
EOU/EPZ Units has been rationalised by counting 
only the physical imports und not the indigenously 
sourced inputs, and allowing, in capital-intensive 
projects, amortisation of capital good, over a lon¬ 
ger period. 

(ix) Refractories, Rubber goods (including moulds fer 
tyre) and glass and glasswares including vacuum 
flask have been included in the US' Of sectors eligi¬ 
ble for import of second-hand capital goods v ithdtit 
a licence. 

Ix) To avoid inconvenience to the manufacturers of 
Ayurvedic and Unani piedicinea, a list of (he crude 
drugs required therefor has been specified. 

f.xi) To promote sports in the country, National/State 
level organisations, associations and eminent Sports- 
persons have been allowed to Import specified cate¬ 
gories of sports goods without a licence. 

(xii) Certain loose ends in the implementation of the 
Duty Exemption Sther**- have been suitably taken 
care of to prevent possible misuse and to facilitate 
easy monitoring of fulfilment of export obligations, 

(xiii) A Complaints Cell ha* been set up in the Headquar¬ 
ters of DGFT to expeditiously attend to complaints 
frftm foreign buyers regarding quality etc. of goods 
exported and to take action Under tho Act, whorcvei 
necessary. 


4. Over (be past years, manv important activities of the 
Oinanfeation have' been computerised in a phased manner 
Continuing our efforts in this direction, it is propped to bring 
the issue of Import-Export Licence-, within the fold of com-' 
putcnsation and it is expected to achieve the same in the 
tumor regional offices of [he Organisation during the nex' 
licensing year, 


-• f 1ie , 'vyiaod edition* of the Export and Import Policv 
Handbook of Procedures and the Standard Input Output norms 
funder Duty Exemption Scheme) have beMt' ptibfflshed in cot- 
changes that have been made Upto the 31st 
March, 1993, These revised editions shall replace’the origi-

CHAPTER 1 
INTRODUCTION 

1. Notification.—In exercise of tile powers conferred 
under section 3 of the Imports and Exports .Control) Act. 
1947 (No. IK cl 1947). the Central Government Jr.td notified 
on 31st March, 1992 the Export and Import Policy for the 
period 1992-97 (hereinafter referred to as the policy). This 
.Act has since been repealed and replaced by the Foreign 
Trade (Development and Regulation) Act, 1992 (No. 22 of 
1992) (here in.dter referred to as the Act), fhi , policy was 
deemed to have been notified under section 5 of the Act. 

2. Application and duration.—This Policy came into force 
with effect from 1st April, 1992 and shall remain tn force 
for a period of five years, that is, upto 31st March, 1997. 

J. Amendment.—The Central Government reserves the

30. Import on re-export basis.—The following capital goods, 
including second-hand capital goods, may be imported on re¬ 
export basis without a licence on execution of bond/bank 
guarantee to the satisfaction of the Customs authorities. 

(a) Capital goods for repairs or reconditioning;

31. Repairs abroad and re-import without Licence.— Impor- 
teu capital goods or paits thereof may be sent abroad for re¬ 
pairs and re-imported without a licence but subject to the 
satisfaction ol the Customs authorities that re-imported 
goods ate the same as the goods that were exported. Export 
of aircraft, its engines and spates for overhauling or repairs 
and their re-import may be made without a licence under this 
pros ision. 

32. Under licence.—Indigenous capital goods having impor¬ 
ted components may be sent abroad for repairs after obtaining 
a licence from the Director General of Foreign Trade for such 
export on re-import basis. 

Indigenous capital goods or parts or components thereof may 
be sent abroad for testing, quality improvement or upgradatio/t 
of technology after obtaining a licence from the Director Gene¬ 
ral of Foteign Trade for such export on re-import basis. 

33. Import of used machinery and equipment.— After com¬ 
pletion of the projects abroad, project contractors may import, 
without a licence, used construction equipment, machinery, rela¬ 
ted spares, tools and accessories on the basis of production of 
evidence of purchase for and use in the overseas project. Used 
office equipment and vehicles may also be imported after com¬ 
pletion of the projects abroad, without a licence, but subject 
to the londibon that they have been used for at least one year. 

34. Import of Gifts.—Import of gifts shall be permitted 
according to the Baggage Rules, 1976. In any other case, a 
Customs Clearance Permit (CCP) shall be required for import 
of gifts by such institutions and establishments as may be 
specified in this behalf. A Customs Clearance Permit (CCP) 
may be issued, on application, by the licensing authority after 
considering the merits of the case. Such imports .will, however, 
be subject to the Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Act, 1916. 

35. Sale on high seas.—Sale of goods on high seas for im¬ 
portation into India may be made subject to this Policy or any 
other law for the time being in force. 

36. Trade with neighbouring countries.—In the case of trade 
with neighbouring countries, the Director General of Foreign 
Trade may issue from time to time such instructions as may be 
required.

CHAPTER VI-A 

EXPORT PROMOTION CAPITAL GOODS SCHEME 
FOR SERVICES SECTOR 

46A. Scheme.—Under the Export Promotion Capital Goods 
Scheme for Services Sector, capital equipment may bo import¬ 
ed for rendering of services for which the payments are re¬ 
ceived in a freely convertible currency. Under the scheme, 
capital equipment (including spares up to 10 per cent of 
the CIF value of the capital equipment) may be imported 
id a concessional rate of custom duty of 15 per cent subject 
to an export obligation of four times the CIF balue; of (he 
imports. The export obligation shall be fulfilled within a 
period of five years from the date of issue of the import 
licence 

46B. Eligibility.—The following categories of service pio- 
\:deis tire eligible to import capital equipment under the 
scheme.

(ii) Artists; 

'.ii) Chartered Accountants; 

(iv) Consultants; 

(v) Diagnostic Centres; 

(vi) Doctors; 

<v\i) Economists; 

i\iii) Engineers;

(x) Journalists;

46C. Items of Import.—The items of capital equipment 
lhat may be permitted to be.' imported under the scheme will 
I a % specified in a Public Notice issued in this bchall. 

The items of capital equipment that may be imported under 
the scheme shall have a diicct nexus with the services ren¬ 
dered for which payments is received in freely convertible 
currency, 

46D. Minimum value of imports.—-An application lot 
grant of a licence under the scheme shall be for a minimum 
CIF value of imports of US $ 10,000. 

46E. Export Obligation.-—The export obligation to be fill- 
idled under the scheme shall be the payments received in a 
fieely convertible currency for the services rendered by the 
licence holder, regardless of whether the services are ten¬ 
dered in India or abroad. The export obligation shall be 
over and above the average love! of hard currency earnings 
of the licence holder, if any, in the preceding three licensing 
years. 

46F. Other Provisions.—-The Provisions of Paiar-iaph 43 
(Procedure for Application) and 45 (LUT and bank guaran¬ 
tee) of this Policy shall apply to this scheme also.

144. Non-profit organisations :—The EPFs are non-profit 
organisations registered under the Companies Act or the 
Societies Registration Act, as the case may be. They are 
supported by financial assistance from the Central Govern¬ 
ment. 

145. Role :—lhe mam role ot lh. n.PCs is to project 
India's image abroad as a reliable supplier of high quality 
joods and services. In particular, the EPCs shall encourage 
and monitor the observance of international standards and 
specifications by exporters. The EPCs shall keep abreast of 
the trends ancl opportunities in international markets for 
goods and services and assist their members in taking advan- 
■age ot such opportunity, in order to expm.d and d.versity 
i nor:. 

1 46. Functions 'The major functions of the EPCs ate as 
follows :— 

(a) To provide commercially useful information and 

assistance to their members in developing and in¬ 
creasing their exports; 

(b) To offer professional advice to their members in 

areas such as technology upgradation, quality and 
design improvement, standards and specifications, 
product development, innovation, etc ,; 

(c) To organise visits of delegations of its membeis 

abroad to explore overseas market opportunities; 

(d) To organise participation in trade fnir3, exhibitions 

and buyer-seller meets in India and abroad ; 

(e) To promote interaction between the cxporling com¬ 

munity and the Government both at the Central 
and State levels; and 

(f) To build a statistical base and provide data on the 
exports and imports of the country, exports and im¬ 
ports of their members, as well as other relevant 
international trade data. 

14/. Membership :—Any exporter may apply to become 
a member of an F.PC and such application shall be consider¬ 
ed end disposed of within one inomli thereof in accordance 
with the rules and regulations of the I PC. On being admit¬ 
ted io membership, the applicant shall be granted forthwith 
a Rcgistration-cum-Membcrvhip Certificate (RCMC). 

148, Professional bodies ‘—In order to play their pBrt m 
Cr promotion of exports, it is important that the EPCs func¬ 
tion as professional bodies. For this purpose, executives with 
a professional background and experience id industry, com¬ 
merce and international marketing should he brought into 
the EPCs. 

149. Aufonomv .—The EPCs would be autonomous and 
shall retaliate their own affairs. -They would not, be required 
to obtain the approval of the Central Government for send¬ 
ing stiles teams or delegations abroad for participation in 
fairs/exhibitions etc. The Central Government would only 
approve the annual plans and budget of the EPCs and moni¬

act with <he Managing Committee of the Council concerned, 
twice a rear, otice for approving the annual plan and budjtei

CHAPTER XIV 
QUALITY 

151. Quality awareness campaign :—It is the policy of the 
Central Government to encourage the manufacturers and ex¬ 
porters ito attain internationally accepted standards of quality 
dor their products. The Central Government will extend 
support and assistance to trade and industry associations to 
launch a nationwide programme on quality awareness and to 
promote the concept of total quality management. 


152 . State-level programmes •-—The Central Government 
will encourage and assist State Governments jn launching a 
similar programme in their i effective States, par ticXtlarly for 
the small scale and handicraft sectors. 


153 Rewards and benefits •—The Central Government will 
introduce a scheme to recognise and suitably reward manu¬ 
facturers who have acquired the ISO 9000 (series) or the BIS 
14000 (series) or any other internationally recognised equiva¬ 
lent certification of quality. Such manufacturers wil] be 
eligible for such benefits as may be notified in this behalf. 

15-1. lest houses :—The Central Go\eminent will ussLt 
in the modernisation and upgrgdntion pf test houses and 
laboratories in order to bring them at pai with international 
standards so that certification by such test houses and la bo- 
i atones is recognised- within the country -and abroad.
